[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is I can’t get my mind off how I look. It sounds a little ridiculous saying it out loud, but I keep thinking my hairline is really noticeable, like it’s receding in this awful way, and my face looks strange—especially under the lights at the restaurant where I work.\n\nI’m around people all the time, you know, customers and coworkers, and those lights hit from above and I feel like they make every line and angle on my face stand out. I’ll catch myself thinking I look old, or odd, or just… not right. And the thing is, people at work have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, like they’ll say, “You look fine,” or “I never noticed that,” but it doesn’t really settle it for me. I still see it. I still feel like it’s obvious. That’s why I’m here, because it’s gotten hard to stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how you look?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. Not in the same way, I guess. It’s hard for me to separate it, because once I start worrying about my appearance, it sort of spreads into everything else—like work, whether people are looking at me, whether I’m coming across weird. But if you mean am I sitting around worrying about money, or my health, or something happening to my family, not really… not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIt mostly keeps coming back to my face and my hairline and how I look under certain lighting. That’s the thing I can’t seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry about how you look been affecting your ability to do your job or interact with others at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. More than I want to admit, honestly. At work it’s become a real problem, not just something in my head that I’m quietly dealing with.\n\nI’ve been late because I’ll get stuck trying to make myself look okay before a shift, or I’ll sit in my car outside the restaurant trying to talk myself into going in, and then suddenly I’m already late. There have been times I just couldn’t face walking in and being under those lights and having people look at me, so I missed the shift. I’ve actually been written up for it, which was embarrassing, because I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding vain or unstable or something.\n\nAnd interacting with people there is harder too. I’m supposed to be the person at the front, greeting people, looking normal and pleasant, and sometimes I feel like I’m just trying to hide my face or angle myself away from the light. I avoid things connected to work too, like staff photos. If someone says, “Let’s get everyone together for a picture,” I’ll find a reason to disappear.\n\nIt’s not just the restaurant either. I’ve stopped going to gallery events and even hikes with people, because I can’t handle the idea of being seen that way or having photos taken. I know that sounds extreme, but in the moment it feels like I’d rather just not go than spend the whole time feeling exposed.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the receding hairline and facial features under certain lighting that bothers you, or is it more about feeling unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more specific than just thinking I’m unattractive overall, I think. I mean, I don’t feel great about how I look in general, but the thing that really grabs hold of me is my hairline and my face under certain lighting—especially at the restaurant.\n\nThose overhead lights make me feel like my hairline looks obviously receded, like it’s the first thing anyone would notice, and then my face looks… I don’t know, harsh or uneven or old in a way that doesn’t look normal. Strange, I guess. And I know people have told me they don’t see it—coworkers have said I look fine or they hadn’t noticed anything—but it doesn’t land for me. I’ll hear them say that and for maybe a second I want to believe it, but then I see myself again under those lights and it’s like, no, there it is.\n\nSo yes, it’s really centered on those things: the hairline, and the way my facial features look when the light hits them. That’s what I can’t stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive, intrusive thoughts about things other than your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’m trying to think if there’s anything else that has that same kind of stuck-on-repeat feeling, and I don’t think so.\n\nI get normal worries, I guess—like if I’m late, or if something at work is going badly—but they don’t feel the same. They don’t keep looping in my head for hours the way the appearance stuff does. It’s not like I’m having intrusive thoughts about, I don’t know, germs or safety or hurting someone or anything like that. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s mostly that once the thought about how I look gets in, it just takes over. Other things might come up around it, like worrying someone noticed or that I looked strange, but it all traces back to my appearance. So… no, I don’t think I have repetitive intrusive thoughts about other things in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social relationships outside of work that you feel have been negatively affected by your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside of work too. It’s definitely affected that.\n\nI’ve stopped going to things I used to at least try to show up for, like gallery events. And I used to go on hikes with people sometimes, which should be low-pressure, you know, just being outside, but even that started feeling impossible because I’d be thinking the whole time about how I looked, whether someone was looking at me from the side, or if there might be photos. If I knew people might take pictures, I’d just find a reason not to go.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because I know it probably comes off like I’m flaky or antisocial, but it’s not that I don’t care about people. It’s more like I can’t handle being seen that way. So I end up avoiding things, and then of course people stop asking as much, or I feel awkward trying to explain why I disappeared. Staff photos at work are part of it too—I know that’s work-related, but it feels social in a way, like everyone laughing and gathering together, and I just can’t make myself stand there and have my face captured like that.\n\nSo yes, relationships have been affected. I’m not showing up for people the way I used to, and I know that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about how you look are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot. I mean, it’s not just that I feel bad when I look in the mirror or something. It’s gotten into my actual life, in ways I’m embarrassed to even say out loud.\n\nAt the restaurant, I’ve been written up because I’ve been late or missed shifts. And it sounds irresponsible, I know, but sometimes I’ll be trying to get ready and I just can’t get past how I look, or I’ll be thinking about those lights and people seeing me, and I freeze or keep trying to fix it until I’m late. Other times I just don’t go, because the thought of standing there where everyone can see my hairline or my face under that lighting feels unbearable.\n\nAnd then there are the things I’ve stopped doing altogether. Staff photos, for example—I avoid those. I can’t handle the idea of being captured like that and then having everyone look at it. And outside of work, I’ve stopped going to gallery events and hikes, things I used to do or at least wanted to do, because I can’t handle being seen that way. I end up making excuses, and then I feel ashamed afterward, but in the moment the distress is so intense that avoiding it feels like the only way to get through it.\n\nSo yes, it causes a lot of distress. It feels like it’s shrinking my life.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your face or hairline that you think is the most unattractive or problematic?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s mostly my hairline, and then how my face looks with it, especially under the lights at the restaurant.\n\nI know that probably sounds vague, but it doesn’t feel vague to me when I’m looking at it. It’s like the hairline makes my whole face look older, harsher, kind of… strange. Like my forehead looks too exposed or uneven, and then under those overhead restaurant lights everything looks worse. My face looks drawn or aged or just not right. I’ll catch myself thinking, “That can’t be how I really look,” and then I’ll look again and it seems even more obvious.\n\nAnd coworkers have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, which almost makes me feel worse in a way, because then I feel like either they’re just being polite or I’m the only one who can see it clearly. But to me it feels very specific. It’s the hairline and the way the lighting hits my face that makes me look old and weird. That’s the part I can’t stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance focused on your hairline and facial features under certain lighting all the time, or does it vary?\n\nPatient: It varies a little, but it’s also kind of always there in the background, if that makes sense. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it — my hairline, and the way my face looks under the restaurant lights. That’s the main thing. I keep feeling like it makes me look old and strange, like something is off about my face.\n\nThe lighting makes it much worse. At the restaurant especially, with those overhead lights, I feel like every flaw is pulled forward, like my hairline is more obvious and my face looks harsher or older. But even when I’m not actually under those lights, I’m still thinking about it. I’ll be anticipating how I’m going to look later, or remembering how I looked the last time, or wondering if other people noticed.\n\nAnd coworkers have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but that doesn’t really calm me down. I just think maybe they’re being nice, or maybe they don’t want to say it. So it’s not exactly every second in the same intensity, but it’s there a lot, and the restaurant lighting is one of the things that really sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships or daily activities because of your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affected both, honestly. At work it’s become a real problem. I’ve been written up at the restaurant because I’m late or I miss shifts, and I hate even saying that because I’m not someone who wants to be unreliable. But sometimes I get stuck trying to get myself looking acceptable enough to go in, or I start thinking about being under the lights and people seeing me, and I just… I either run late or I can’t make myself go.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve stopped doing things I used to at least try to do. Staff photos are a big one—I avoid them because the idea of everyone seeing a picture of me feels awful. And outside work, I’ve stopped going to gallery events and hikes. It sounds like small stuff maybe, but those were things that connected me to people and to my life, and now I make excuses because I can’t handle being seen that way. Then afterward I feel embarrassed and ashamed, but in the moment avoiding it feels easier than walking in and feeling exposed.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about how you look mostly when you're around other people or can it happen when you're alone too?\n\nPatient: It happens when I’m alone too. It’s definitely worse around other people, or when I know I’m going to be seen under those lights at the restaurant, but it doesn’t just switch off when I’m by myself.\n\nI’ll be alone and still thinking about my hairline, or picturing how my face looks in that lighting, and I get stuck on the idea that it makes me look old and strange. Even if nobody is there looking at me, I’m sort of… looking at myself in my head, if that makes sense. And then when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I know I should feel reassured, but I don’t. I keep thinking maybe they just don’t want to say it, or maybe it’s obvious in certain lighting and they’re missing it.\n\nSo yes, people being around makes it more intense, but the worry is there even when I’m alone. It follows me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance might be exaggerated or not entirely based on how you actually look?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might be exaggerated, because people at work have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about. My coworkers will say my hairline looks normal, or that my face doesn’t look old or strange under the lights the way I think it does. So I can understand, logically, that maybe I’m not seeing it the way other people are.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel that way to me. It feels very real when I’m looking at myself or even just thinking about being under those restaurant lights. I get stuck on the idea that my hairline is obvious and my face looks wrong somehow, like older or harsher or just strange. And even when someone tells me they don’t notice it, I don’t really believe it fully. I start thinking, well, maybe they’re just being kind, or maybe they’re not looking closely, or maybe it only shows in that specific lighting.\n\nSo yes, I guess I can admit there’s a chance I’m making it bigger than it is… but I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it. It’s not like I can just tell myself, “That’s exaggerated,” and then it goes away. It stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hairline and face have changed at all over time, or have they stayed pretty much the same?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s hard to say they’ve changed in a clear way, because the focus is still the same. It keeps coming back to my hairline and my face, especially how my face looks under the restaurant lights. That’s the thing I can’t seem to get away from.\n\nI’ll think, “My hairline looks too noticeable,” or “My face looks old under those lights,” or not even just old—strange, like something is off in a way people can see. I might feel relieved for a minute, but then I start questioning it again, like maybe they’re just being polite or maybe they didn’t see me from the right angle.\n\nSo I guess over time it hasn’t gone away or shifted into something else. It’s stayed centered on that same fear: that my hairline and my face make me look old and strange, and I just can’t stop thinking about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And it sounds irresponsible, I know, but sometimes I’ll be trying to get ready and I just can’t get past how I look, or I’ll be thinking about those lights and people seeing me, and I freeze or keep trying to fix it until I’m late. Other times I just don’t go, because the thought of standing there where everyone can see my hairline or my face under that lighting feels unbearable.\n\nAnd then there are the things I’ve stopped doing altogether. Staff photos, for example—I avoid those. I can’t handle the idea of being captured like that and then having everyone look at it. And outside of work, I’ve stopped going to gallery events and hikes, things I used to do or at least wanted to do, because I can’t handle being seen that way. I end up making excuses, and then I feel ashamed afterward, but in the moment the distress is so intense that avoiding it feels like the only way to get through it.\n\nSo yes, it causes a lot of distress. It feels like it’s shrinking my life.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your face or hairline that you think is the most unattractive or problematic?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s mostly my hairline, and then how my face looks with it, especially under the lights at the restaurant.\n\nI know that probably sounds vague, but it doesn’t feel vague to me when I’m looking at it. It’s like the hairline makes my whole face look older, harsher, kind of… strange. Like my forehead looks too exposed or uneven, and then under those overhead restaurant lights everything looks worse. My face looks drawn or aged or just not right. I’ll catch myself thinking, “That can’t be how I really look,” and then I’ll look again and it seems even more obvious.\n\nAnd coworkers have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, which almost makes me feel worse in a way, because then I feel like either they’re just being polite or I’m the only one who can see it clearly. But to me it feels very specific. It’s the hairline and the way the lighting hits my face that makes me look old and weird. That’s the part I can’t stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance focused on your hairline and facial features under certain lighting all the time, or does it vary?\n\nPatient: It varies a little, but it’s also kind of always there in the background, if that makes sense. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it — my hairline, and the way my face looks under the restaurant lights. That’s the main thing. I keep feeling like it makes me look old and strange, like something is off about my face.\n\nThe lighting makes it much worse. At the restaurant especially, with those overhead lights, I feel like every flaw is pulled forward, like my hairline is more obvious and my face looks harsher or older. But even when I’m not actually under those lights, I’m still thinking about it. I’ll be anticipating how I’m going to look later, or remembering how I looked the last time, or wondering if other people noticed.\n\nAnd coworkers have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but that doesn’t really calm me down. I just think maybe they’re being nice, or maybe they don’t want to say it. So it’s not exactly every second in the same intensity, but it’s there a lot, and the restaurant lighting is one of the things that really sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships or daily activities because of your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affected both, honestly. At work it’s become a real problem. I’ve been written up at the restaurant because I’m late or I miss shifts, and I hate even saying that because I’m not someone who wants to be unreliable. But sometimes I get stuck trying to get myself looking acceptable enough to go in, or I start thinking about being under the lights and people seeing me, and I just… I either run late or I can’t make myself go.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve stopped doing things I used to at least try to do. Staff photos are a big one—I avoid them because the idea of everyone seeing a picture of me feels awful. And outside work, I’ve stopped going to gallery events and hikes. It sounds like small stuff maybe, but those were things that connected me to people and to my life, and now I make excuses because I can’t handle being seen that way. Then afterward I feel embarrassed and ashamed, but in the moment avoiding it feels easier than walking in and feeling exposed.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about how you look mostly when you're around other people or can it happen when you're alone too?\n\nPatient: It happens when I’m alone too. It’s definitely worse around other people, or when I know I’m going to be seen under those lights at the restaurant, but it doesn’t just switch off when I’m by myself.\n\nI’ll be alone and still thinking about my hairline, or picturing how my face looks in that lighting, and I get stuck on the idea that it makes me look old and strange. Even if nobody is there looking at me, I’m sort of… looking at myself in my head, if that makes sense. And then when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I know I should feel reassured, but I don’t. I keep thinking maybe they just don’t want to say it, or maybe it’s obvious in certain lighting and they’re missing it.\n\nSo yes, people being around makes it more intense, but the worry is there even when I’m alone. It follows me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance might be exaggerated or not entirely based on how you actually look?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might be exaggerated, because people at work have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about. My coworkers will say my hairline looks normal, or that my face doesn’t look old or strange under the lights the way I think it does. So I can understand, logically, that maybe I’m not seeing it the way other people are.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel that way to me. It feels very real when I’m looking at myself or even just thinking about being under those restaurant lights. I get stuck on the idea that my hairline is obvious and my face looks wrong somehow, like older or harsher or just strange. And even when someone tells me they don’t notice it, I don’t really believe it fully. I start thinking, well, maybe they’re just being kind, or maybe they’re not looking closely, or maybe it only shows in that specific lighting.\n\nSo yes, I guess I can admit there’s a chance I’m making it bigger than it is… but I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it. It’s not like I can just tell myself, “That’s exaggerated,” and then it goes away. It stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hairline and face have changed at all over time, or have they stayed pretty much the same?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s hard to say they’ve changed in a clear way, because the focus is still the same. It keeps coming back to my hairline and my face, especially how my face looks under the restaurant lights. That’s the thing I can’t seem to get away from.\n\nI’ll think, “My hairline looks too noticeable,” or “My face looks old under those lights,” or not even just old—strange, like something is off in a way people can see. And even when coworkers tell me they don’t see it, or they say I look fine, it doesn’t really settle it for me. I might feel relieved for a minute, but then I start questioning it again, like maybe they’re just being polite or maybe they didn’t see me from the right angle.\n\nSo I guess over time it hasn’t gone away or shifted into something else. It’s stayed centered on that same fear: that my hairline and my face make me look old and strange, and I just can’t stop thinking about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I mean, I suppose there is a possibility. I can say that intellectually, because my coworkers keep telling me they don’t see what I’m talking about. They’ll say my hairline looks normal, or that my face doesn’t look old or strange under the restaurant lights. So I know there’s some chance that what I’m seeing isn’t what other people are seeing.

But it doesn’t feel like just a possibility when I’m in it. It feels obvious to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking that my hairline and the way my face looks under those lights make me look old and strange, even when people around me say they don’t notice anything. I’ll try to take in what they’re saying, but then I start wondering if they’re just being nice, or if they didn’t see me at the right angle, or if maybe the lighting was different that moment.

So… yes, maybe my perception is different from reality, or different from how others see me. But I don’t trust that enough for it to calm me down. The thought just keeps coming back.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is I can’t get my mind off how I look. It sounds a little ridiculous saying it out loud, but I keep thinking my hairline is really noticeable, like it’s receding in this awful way, and my face looks strange—especially under the lights at the restaurant where I work.\n\nI’m around people all the time, you know, customers and coworkers, and those lights hit from above and I feel like they make every line and angle on my face stand out. I’ll catch myself thinking I look old, or odd, or just… not right. I still see it. I still feel like it’s obvious. That’s why I’m here, because it’s gotten hard to stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how you look?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. Not in the same way, I guess. It’s hard for me to separate it, because once I start worrying about my appearance, it sort of spreads into everything else—like work, whether people are looking at me, whether I’m coming across weird. But if you mean am I sitting around worrying about money, or my health, or something happening to my family, not really… not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIt mostly keeps coming back to my face and my hairline and how I look under certain lighting. That’s the thing I can’t seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry about how you look been affecting your ability to do your job or interact with others at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. More than I want to admit, honestly. At work it’s become a real problem, not just something in my head that I’m quietly dealing with.\n\nI’ve been late because I’ll get stuck trying to make myself look okay before a shift, or I’ll sit in my car outside the restaurant trying to talk myself into going in, and then suddenly I’m already late. There have been times I just couldn’t face walking in and being under those lights and having people look at me, so I missed the shift. I’ve actually been written up for it, which was embarrassing, because I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding vain or unstable or something.\n\nAnd interacting with people there is harder too. I’m supposed to be the person at the front, greeting people, looking normal and pleasant, and sometimes I feel like I’m just trying to hide my face or angle myself away from the light. I avoid things connected to work too, like staff photos. If someone says, “Let’s get everyone together for a picture,” I’ll find a reason to disappear.\n\nIt’s not just the restaurant either. I’ve stopped going to gallery events and even hikes with people, because I can’t handle the idea of being seen that way or having photos taken. I know that sounds extreme, but in the moment it feels like I’d rather just not go than spend the whole time feeling exposed.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the receding hairline and facial features under certain lighting that bothers you, or is it more about feeling unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more specific than just thinking I’m unattractive overall, I think. I mean, I don’t feel great about how I look in general, but the thing that really grabs hold of me is my hairline and my face under certain lighting—especially at the restaurant.\n\nThose overhead lights make me feel like my hairline looks obviously receded, like it’s the first thing anyone would notice, and then my face looks… I don’t know, harsh or uneven or old in a way that doesn’t look normal. Strange, I guess. And I know people have told me they don’t see it—coworkers have said I look fine or they hadn’t noticed anything—but it doesn’t land for me. I’ll hear them say that and for maybe a second I want to believe it, but then I see myself again under those lights and it’s like, no, there it is.\n\nSo yes, it’s really centered on those things: the hairline, and the way my facial features look when the light hits them. That’s what I can’t stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive, intrusive thoughts about things other than your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’m trying to think if there’s anything else that has that same kind of stuck-on-repeat feeling, and I don’t think so.\n\nI get normal worries, I guess—like if I’m late, or if something at work is going badly—but they don’t feel the same. They don’t keep looping in my head for hours the way the appearance stuff does. It’s not like I’m having intrusive thoughts about, I don’t know, germs or safety or hurting someone or anything like that. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s mostly that once the thought about how I look gets in, it just takes over. Other things might come up around it, like worrying someone noticed or that I looked strange, but it all traces back to my appearance. So… no, I don’t think I have repetitive intrusive thoughts about other things in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social relationships outside of work that you feel have been negatively affected by your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside of work too. It’s definitely affected that.\n\nI’ve stopped going to things I used to at least try to show up for, like gallery events. And I used to go on hikes with people sometimes, which should be low-pressure, you know, just being outside, but even that started feeling impossible because I’d be thinking the whole time about how I looked, whether someone was looking at me from the side, or if there might be photos. If I knew people might take pictures, I’d just find a reason not to go.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because I know it probably comes off like I’m flaky or antisocial, but it’s not that I don’t care about people. It’s more like I can’t handle being seen that way. So I end up avoiding things, and then of course people stop asking as much, or I feel awkward trying to explain why I disappeared. Staff photos at work are part of it too—I know that’s work-related, but it feels social in a way, like everyone laughing and gathering together, and I just can’t make myself stand there and have my face captured like that.\n\nSo yes, relationships have been affected. I’m not showing up for people the way I used to, and I know that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about how you look are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot. I mean, it’s not just that I feel bad when I look in the mirror or something. It’s gotten into my actual life, in ways I’m embarrassed to even say out loud.\n\nAt the restaurant, I’ve been written up because I’ve been late or missed shifts. And it sounds irresponsible, I know, but sometimes I’ll be trying to get ready and I just can’t get past how I look, or I’ll be thinking about those lights and people seeing me, and I freeze or keep trying to fix it until I’m late. Other times I just don’t go, because the thought of standing there where everyone can see my hairline or my face under that lighting feels unbearable.\n\nAnd then there are the things I’ve stopped doing altogether. Staff photos, for example—I avoid those. I can’t handle the idea of being captured like that and then having everyone look at it. And outside of work, I’ve stopped going to gallery events and hikes, things I used to do or at least wanted to do, because I can’t handle being seen that way. I end up making excuses, and then I feel ashamed afterward, but in the moment the distress is so intense that avoiding it feels like the only way to get through it.\n\nSo yes, it causes a lot of distress. It feels like it’s shrinking my life.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your face or hairline that you think is the most unattractive or problematic?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s mostly my hairline, and then how my face looks with it, especially under the lights at the restaurant.\n\nI know that probably sounds vague, but it doesn’t feel vague to me when I’m looking at it. It’s like the hairline makes my whole face look older, harsher, kind of… strange. Like my forehead looks too exposed or uneven, and then under those overhead restaurant lights everything looks worse. My face looks drawn or aged or just not right. I’ll catch myself thinking, “That can’t be how I really look,” and then I’ll look again and it seems even more obvious.\n\nAnd coworkers have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, which almost makes me feel worse in a way, because then I feel like either they’re just being polite or I’m the only one who can see it clearly. But to me it feels very specific. It’s the hairline and the way the lighting hits my face that makes me look old and weird. That’s the part I can’t stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance focused on your hairline and facial features under certain lighting all the time, or does it vary?\n\nPatient: It varies a little, but it’s also kind of always there in the background, if that makes sense. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it — my hairline, and the way my face looks under the restaurant lights. That’s the main thing. I keep feeling like it makes me look old and strange, like something is off about my face.\n\nThe lighting makes it much worse. At the restaurant especially, with those overhead lights, I feel like every flaw is pulled forward, like my hairline is more obvious and my face looks harsher or older. But even when I’m not actually under those lights, I’m still thinking about it. I’ll be anticipating how I’m going to look later, or remembering how I looked the last time, or wondering if other people noticed.\n\nAnd coworkers have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but that doesn’t really calm me down. I just think maybe they’re being nice, or maybe they don’t want to say it. So it’s not exactly every second in the same intensity, but it’s there a lot, and the restaurant lighting is one of the things that really sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships or daily activities because of your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affected both, honestly. At work it’s become a real problem. I’ve been written up at the restaurant because I’m late or I miss shifts, and I hate even saying that because I’m not someone who wants to be unreliable. But sometimes I get stuck trying to get myself looking acceptable enough to go in, or I start thinking about being under the lights and people seeing me, and I just… I either run late or I can’t make myself go.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve stopped doing things I used to at least try to do. Staff photos are a big one—I avoid them because the idea of everyone seeing a picture of me feels awful. And outside work, I’ve stopped going to gallery events and hikes. It sounds like small stuff maybe, but those were things that connected me to people and to my life, and now I make excuses because I can’t handle being seen that way. Then afterward I feel embarrassed and ashamed, but in the moment avoiding it feels easier than walking in and feeling exposed.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about how you look mostly when you're around other people or can it happen when you're alone too?\n\nPatient: It happens when I’m alone too. It’s definitely worse around other people, or when I know I’m going to be seen under those lights at the restaurant, but it doesn’t just switch off when I’m by myself.\n\nI’ll be alone and still thinking about my hairline, or picturing how my face looks in that lighting, and I get stuck on the idea that it makes me look old and strange. Even if nobody is there looking at me, I’m sort of… looking at myself in my head, if that makes sense. And then when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I know I should feel reassured, but I don’t. I keep thinking maybe they just don’t want to say it, or maybe it’s obvious in certain lighting and they’re missing it.\n\nSo yes, people being around makes it more intense, but the worry is there even when I’m alone. It follows me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance might be exaggerated or not entirely based on how you actually look?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might be exaggerated, because people at work have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about. My coworkers will say my hairline looks normal, or that my face doesn’t look old or strange under the lights the way I think it does. So I can understand, logically, that maybe I’m not seeing it the way other people are.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel that way to me. It feels very real when I’m looking at myself or even just thinking about being under those restaurant lights. I get stuck on the idea that my hairline is obvious and my face looks wrong somehow, like older or harsher or just strange. And even when someone tells me they don’t notice it, I don’t really believe it fully. I start thinking, well, maybe they’re just being kind, or maybe they’re not looking closely, or maybe it only shows in that specific lighting.\n\nSo yes, I guess I can admit there’s a chance I’m making it bigger than it is… but I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it. It’s not like I can just tell myself, “That’s exaggerated,” and then it goes away. It stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hairline and face have changed at all over time, or have they stayed pretty much the same?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s hard to say they’ve changed in a clear way, because the focus is still the same. It keeps coming back to my hairline and my face, especially how my face looks under the restaurant lights. That’s the thing I can’t seem to get away from.\n\nI’ll think, “My hairline looks too noticeable,” or “My face looks old under those lights,” or not even just old—strange, like something is off in a way people can see. And even when coworkers tell me they don’t see it, or they say I look fine, it doesn’t really settle it for me. I might feel relieved for a minute, but then I start questioning it again, like maybe they’re just being polite or maybe they didn’t see me from the right angle.\n\nSo I guess over time it hasn’t gone away or shifted into something else. It’s stayed centered on that same fear: that my hairline and my face make me look old and strange, and I just can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your perception of your hairline and face might be different from how they actually appear to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose there is a possibility. I can say that intellectually, because my coworkers keep telling me they don’t see what I’m talking about. They’ll say my hairline looks normal, or that my face doesn’t look old or strange under the restaurant lights. So I know there’s some chance that what I’m seeing isn’t what other people are seeing.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like just a possibility when I’m in it. It feels obvious to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking that my hairline and the way my face looks under those lights make me look old and strange, even when people around me say they don’t notice anything. I’ll try to take in what they’re saying, but then I start wondering if they’re just being nice, or if they didn’t see me at the right angle, or if maybe the lighting was different that moment.\n\nSo… yes, maybe my perception is different from reality, or different from how others see me. But I don’t trust that enough for it to calm me down. The thought just keeps coming back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s probably the main reason I’m here, honestly. I feel like I can’t control it once it starts. I’ll try to tell myself to stop checking or stop thinking about it, but then my mind goes right back to my hairline, or to how my face looks under the restaurant lights.

It’s like I get stuck on this idea that I look old or strange, and even if my coworkers say, “No, you look fine,” or “I don’t see what you’re talking about,” it doesn’t really shut it off. I might hear them and want to believe them, but then a few minutes later I’m thinking, “Maybe they just didn’t notice,” or “Maybe the lighting wasn’t the same,” or “Maybe they’re trying not to hurt my feelings.”

So, yes… it feels very hard to control. It’s not just a passing thought. It keeps coming back, and I can’t seem to leave it alone.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is I can’t get my mind off how I look. It sounds a little ridiculous saying it out loud, but I keep thinking my hairline is really noticeable, like it’s receding in this awful way, and my face looks strange—especially under the lights at the restaurant where I work.\n\nI’m around people all the time, you know, customers and coworkers, and those lights hit from above and I feel like they make every line and angle on my face stand out. I’ll catch myself thinking I look old, or odd, or just… not right. And the thing is, people at work have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, like they’ll say, “You look fine,” or “I never noticed that,” but it doesn’t really settle it for me. I still see it. I still feel like it’s obvious. That’s why I’m here, because it’s gotten hard to stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about anything else besides how you look?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. Not in the same way, I guess. It’s hard for me to separate it, because once I start worrying about my appearance, it sort of spreads into everything else—like work, whether people are looking at me, whether I’m coming across weird. But if you mean am I sitting around worrying about money, or my health, or something happening to my family, not really… not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIt mostly keeps coming back to my face and my hairline and how I look under certain lighting. That’s the thing I can’t seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Has your worry about how you look been affecting your ability to do your job or interact with others at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has. More than I want to admit, honestly. At work it’s become a real problem, not just something in my head that I’m quietly dealing with.\n\nI’ve been late because I’ll get stuck trying to make myself look okay before a shift, or I’ll sit in my car outside the restaurant trying to talk myself into going in, and then suddenly I’m already late. There have been times I just couldn’t face walking in and being under those lights and having people look at me, so I missed the shift. I’ve actually been written up for it, which was embarrassing, because I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding vain or unstable or something.\n\nAnd interacting with people there is harder too. I’m supposed to be the person at the front, greeting people, looking normal and pleasant, and sometimes I feel like I’m just trying to hide my face or angle myself away from the light. I avoid things connected to work too, like staff photos. If someone says, “Let’s get everyone together for a picture,” I’ll find a reason to disappear.\n\nIt’s not just the restaurant either. I’ve stopped going to gallery events and even hikes with people, because I can’t handle the idea of being seen that way or having photos taken. I know that sounds extreme, but in the moment it feels like I’d rather just not go than spend the whole time feeling exposed.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the receding hairline and facial features under certain lighting that bothers you, or is it more about feeling unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more specific than just thinking I’m unattractive overall, I think. I mean, I don’t feel great about how I look in general, but the thing that really grabs hold of me is my hairline and my face under certain lighting—especially at the restaurant.\n\nThose overhead lights make me feel like my hairline looks obviously receded, like it’s the first thing anyone would notice, and then my face looks… I don’t know, harsh or uneven or old in a way that doesn’t look normal. Strange, I guess. And I know people have told me they don’t see it—coworkers have said I look fine or they hadn’t noticed anything—but it doesn’t land for me. I’ll hear them say that and for maybe a second I want to believe it, but then I see myself again under those lights and it’s like, no, there it is.\n\nSo yes, it’s really centered on those things: the hairline, and the way my facial features look when the light hits them. That’s what I can’t stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive, intrusive thoughts about things other than your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’m trying to think if there’s anything else that has that same kind of stuck-on-repeat feeling, and I don’t think so.\n\nI get normal worries, I guess—like if I’m late, or if something at work is going badly—but they don’t feel the same. They don’t keep looping in my head for hours the way the appearance stuff does. It’s not like I’m having intrusive thoughts about, I don’t know, germs or safety or hurting someone or anything like that. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s mostly that once the thought about how I look gets in, it just takes over. Other things might come up around it, like worrying someone noticed or that I looked strange, but it all traces back to my appearance. So… no, I don’t think I have repetitive intrusive thoughts about other things in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social relationships outside of work that you feel have been negatively affected by your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside of work too. It’s definitely affected that.\n\nI’ve stopped going to things I used to at least try to show up for, like gallery events. And I used to go on hikes with people sometimes, which should be low-pressure, you know, just being outside, but even that started feeling impossible because I’d be thinking the whole time about how I looked, whether someone was looking at me from the side, or if there might be photos. If I knew people might take pictures, I’d just find a reason not to go.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because I know it probably comes off like I’m flaky or antisocial, but it’s not that I don’t care about people. It’s more like I can’t handle being seen that way. So I end up avoiding things, and then of course people stop asking as much, or I feel awkward trying to explain why I disappeared. Staff photos at work are part of it too—I know that’s work-related, but it feels social in a way, like everyone laughing and gathering together, and I just can’t make myself stand there and have my face captured like that.\n\nSo yes, relationships have been affected. I’m not showing up for people the way I used to, and I know that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about how you look are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot. I mean, it’s not just that I feel bad when I look in the mirror or something. It’s gotten into my actual life, in ways I’m embarrassed to even say out loud.\n\nAt the restaurant, I’ve been written up because I’ve been late or missed shifts. And it sounds irresponsible, I know, but sometimes I’ll be trying to get ready and I just can’t get past how I look, or I’ll be thinking about those lights and people seeing me, and I freeze or keep trying to fix it until I’m late. Other times I just don’t go, because the thought of standing there where everyone can see my hairline or my face under that lighting feels unbearable.\n\nAnd then there are the things I’ve stopped doing altogether. Staff photos, for example—I avoid those. I can’t handle the idea of being captured like that and then having everyone look at it. And outside of work, I’ve stopped going to gallery events and hikes, things I used to do or at least wanted to do, because I can’t handle being seen that way. I end up making excuses, and then I feel ashamed afterward, but in the moment the distress is so intense that avoiding it feels like the only way to get through it.\n\nSo yes, it causes a lot of distress. It feels like it’s shrinking my life.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your face or hairline that you think is the most unattractive or problematic?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s mostly my hairline, and then how my face looks with it, especially under the lights at the restaurant.\n\nI know that probably sounds vague, but it doesn’t feel vague to me when I’m looking at it. It’s like the hairline makes my whole face look older, harsher, kind of… strange. Like my forehead looks too exposed or uneven, and then under those overhead restaurant lights everything looks worse. My face looks drawn or aged or just not right. I’ll catch myself thinking, “That can’t be how I really look,” and then I’ll look again and it seems even more obvious.\n\nAnd coworkers have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, which almost makes me feel worse in a way, because then I feel like either they’re just being polite or I’m the only one who can see it clearly. But to me it feels very specific. It’s the hairline and the way the lighting hits my face that makes me look old and weird. That’s the part I can’t stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance focused on your hairline and facial features under certain lighting all the time, or does it vary?\n\nPatient: It varies a little, but it’s also kind of always there in the background, if that makes sense. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it — my hairline, and the way my face looks under the restaurant lights. That’s the main thing. I keep feeling like it makes me look old and strange, like something is off about my face.\n\nThe lighting makes it much worse. At the restaurant especially, with those overhead lights, I feel like every flaw is pulled forward, like my hairline is more obvious and my face looks harsher or older. But even when I’m not actually under those lights, I’m still thinking about it. I’ll be anticipating how I’m going to look later, or remembering how I looked the last time, or wondering if other people noticed.\n\nAnd coworkers have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but that doesn’t really calm me down. I just think maybe they’re being nice, or maybe they don’t want to say it. So it’s not exactly every second in the same intensity, but it’s there a lot, and the restaurant lighting is one of the things that really sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships or daily activities because of your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affected both, honestly. At work it’s become a real problem. I’ve been written up at the restaurant because I’m late or I miss shifts, and I hate even saying that because I’m not someone who wants to be unreliable. But sometimes I get stuck trying to get myself looking acceptable enough to go in, or I start thinking about being under the lights and people seeing me, and I just… I either run late or I can’t make myself go.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve stopped doing things I used to at least try to do. Staff photos are a big one—I avoid them because the idea of everyone seeing a picture of me feels awful. And outside work, I’ve stopped going to gallery events and hikes. It sounds like small stuff maybe, but those were things that connected me to people and to my life, and now I make excuses because I can’t handle being seen that way. Then afterward I feel embarrassed and ashamed, but in the moment avoiding it feels easier than walking in and feeling exposed.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about how you look mostly when you're around other people or can it happen when you're alone too?\n\nPatient: It happens when I’m alone too. It’s definitely worse around other people, or when I know I’m going to be seen under those lights at the restaurant, but it doesn’t just switch off when I’m by myself.\n\nI’ll be alone and still thinking about my hairline, or picturing how my face looks in that lighting, and I get stuck on the idea that it makes me look old and strange. Even if nobody is there looking at me, I’m sort of… looking at myself in my head, if that makes sense. And then when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I know I should feel reassured, but I don’t. I keep thinking maybe they just don’t want to say it, or maybe it’s obvious in certain lighting and they’re missing it.\n\nSo yes, people being around makes it more intense, but the worry is there even when I’m alone. It follows me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance might be exaggerated or not entirely based on how you actually look?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might be exaggerated, because people at work have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about. My coworkers will say my hairline looks normal, or that my face doesn’t look old or strange under the lights the way I think it does. So I can understand, logically, that maybe I’m not seeing it the way other people are.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel that way to me. It feels very real when I’m looking at myself or even just thinking about being under those restaurant lights. I get stuck on the idea that my hairline is obvious and my face looks wrong somehow, like older or harsher or just strange. And even when someone tells me they don’t notice it, I don’t really believe it fully. I start thinking, well, maybe they’re just being kind, or maybe they’re not looking closely, or maybe it only shows in that specific lighting.\n\nSo yes, I guess I can admit there’s a chance I’m making it bigger than it is… but I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it. It’s not like I can just tell myself, “That’s exaggerated,” and then it goes away. It stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hairline and face have changed at all over time, or have they stayed pretty much the same?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s hard to say they’ve changed in a clear way, because the focus is still the same. I’ve stopped going to gallery events and even hikes with people, because I can’t handle the idea of being seen that way or having photos taken. I know that sounds extreme, but in the moment it feels like I’d rather just not go than spend the whole time feeling exposed.\n\nDoctor: Is it specifically the receding hairline and facial features under certain lighting that bothers you, or is it more about feeling unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more specific than just thinking I’m unattractive overall, I think. I mean, I don’t feel great about how I look in general, but the thing that really grabs hold of me is my hairline and my face under certain lighting—especially at the restaurant.\n\nThose overhead lights make me feel like my hairline looks obviously receded, like it’s the first thing anyone would notice, and then my face looks… I don’t know, harsh or uneven or old in a way that doesn’t look normal. Strange, I guess. And I know people have told me they don’t see it—coworkers have said I look fine or they hadn’t noticed anything—but it doesn’t land for me. I’ll hear them say that and for maybe a second I want to believe it, but then I see myself again under those lights and it’s like, no, there it is.\n\nSo yes, it’s really centered on those things: the hairline, and the way my facial features look when the light hits them. That’s what I can’t stop thinking about.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive, intrusive thoughts about things other than your appearance?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’m trying to think if there’s anything else that has that same kind of stuck-on-repeat feeling, and I don’t think so.\n\nI get normal worries, I guess—like if I’m late, or if something at work is going badly—but they don’t feel the same. They don’t keep looping in my head for hours the way the appearance stuff does. It’s not like I’m having intrusive thoughts about, I don’t know, germs or safety or hurting someone or anything like that. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nIt’s mostly that once the thought about how I look gets in, it just takes over. Other things might come up around it, like worrying someone noticed or that I looked strange, but it all traces back to my appearance. So… no, I don’t think I have repetitive intrusive thoughts about other things in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social relationships outside of work that you feel have been negatively affected by your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah… outside of work too. It’s definitely affected that.\n\nI’ve stopped going to things I used to at least try to show up for, like gallery events. And I used to go on hikes with people sometimes, which should be low-pressure, you know, just being outside, but even that started feeling impossible because I’d be thinking the whole time about how I looked, whether someone was looking at me from the side, or if there might be photos. If I knew people might take pictures, I’d just find a reason not to go.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because I know it probably comes off like I’m flaky or antisocial, but it’s not that I don’t care about people. It’s more like I can’t handle being seen that way. So I end up avoiding things, and then of course people stop asking as much, or I feel awkward trying to explain why I disappeared. Staff photos at work are part of it too—I know that’s work-related, but it feels social in a way, like everyone laughing and gathering together, and I just can’t make myself stand there and have my face captured like that.\n\nSo yes, relationships have been affected. I’m not showing up for people the way I used to, and I know that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your concerns about how you look are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. A lot. I mean, it’s not just that I feel bad when I look in the mirror or something. It’s gotten into my actual life, in ways I’m embarrassed to even say out loud.\n\nAt the restaurant, I’ve been written up because I’ve been late or missed shifts. And it sounds irresponsible, I know, but sometimes I’ll be trying to get ready and I just can’t get past how I look, or I’ll be thinking about those lights and people seeing me, and I freeze or keep trying to fix it until I’m late. Other times I just don’t go, because the thought of standing there where everyone can see my hairline or my face under that lighting feels unbearable.\n\nAnd then there are the things I’ve stopped doing altogether. Staff photos, for example—I avoid those. I can’t handle the idea of being captured like that and then having everyone look at it. And outside of work, I’ve stopped going to gallery events and hikes, things I used to do or at least wanted to do, because I can’t handle being seen that way. I end up making excuses, and then I feel ashamed afterward, but in the moment the distress is so intense that avoiding it feels like the only way to get through it.\n\nSo yes, it causes a lot of distress. It feels like it’s shrinking my life.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your face or hairline that you think is the most unattractive or problematic?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s mostly my hairline, and then how my face looks with it, especially under the lights at the restaurant.\n\nI know that probably sounds vague, but it doesn’t feel vague to me when I’m looking at it. It’s like the hairline makes my whole face look older, harsher, kind of… strange. Like my forehead looks too exposed or uneven, and then under those overhead restaurant lights everything looks worse. My face looks drawn or aged or just not right. I’ll catch myself thinking, “That can’t be how I really look,” and then I’ll look again and it seems even more obvious.\n\nAnd coworkers have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, which almost makes me feel worse in a way, because then I feel like either they’re just being polite or I’m the only one who can see it clearly. But to me it feels very specific. It’s the hairline and the way the lighting hits my face that makes me look old and weird. That’s the part I can’t stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance focused on your hairline and facial features under certain lighting all the time, or does it vary?\n\nPatient: It varies a little, but it’s also kind of always there in the background, if that makes sense. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it — my hairline, and the way my face looks under the restaurant lights. That’s the main thing. I keep feeling like it makes me look old and strange, like something is off about my face.\n\nThe lighting makes it much worse. At the restaurant especially, with those overhead lights, I feel like every flaw is pulled forward, like my hairline is more obvious and my face looks harsher or older. But even when I’m not actually under those lights, I’m still thinking about it. I’ll be anticipating how I’m going to look later, or remembering how I looked the last time, or wondering if other people noticed.\n\nAnd coworkers have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but that doesn’t really calm me down. I just think maybe they’re being nice, or maybe they don’t want to say it. So it’s not exactly every second in the same intensity, but it’s there a lot, and the restaurant lighting is one of the things that really sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships or daily activities because of your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affected both, honestly. At work it’s become a real problem. I’ve been written up at the restaurant because I’m late or I miss shifts, and I hate even saying that because I’m not someone who wants to be unreliable. But sometimes I get stuck trying to get myself looking acceptable enough to go in, or I start thinking about being under the lights and people seeing me, and I just… I either run late or I can’t make myself go.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve stopped doing things I used to at least try to do. Staff photos are a big one—I avoid them because the idea of everyone seeing a picture of me feels awful. And outside work, I’ve stopped going to gallery events and hikes. It sounds like small stuff maybe, but those were things that connected me to people and to my life, and now I make excuses because I can’t handle being seen that way. Then afterward I feel embarrassed and ashamed, but in the moment avoiding it feels easier than walking in and feeling exposed.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about how you look mostly when you're around other people or can it happen when you're alone too?\n\nPatient: It happens when I’m alone too. It’s definitely worse around other people, or when I know I’m going to be seen under those lights at the restaurant, but it doesn’t just switch off when I’m by myself.\n\nI’ll be alone and still thinking about my hairline, or picturing how my face looks in that lighting, and I get stuck on the idea that it makes me look old and strange. Even if nobody is there looking at me, I’m sort of… looking at myself in my head, if that makes sense. And then when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I know I should feel reassured, but I don’t. I keep thinking maybe they just don’t want to say it, or maybe it’s obvious in certain lighting and they’re missing it.\n\nSo yes, people being around makes it more intense, but the worry is there even when I’m alone. It follows me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance might be exaggerated or not entirely based on how you actually look?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might be exaggerated, because people at work have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about. My coworkers will say my hairline looks normal, or that my face doesn’t look old or strange under the lights the way I think it does. So I can understand, logically, that maybe I’m not seeing it the way other people are.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel that way to me. It feels very real when I’m looking at myself or even just thinking about being under those restaurant lights. I get stuck on the idea that my hairline is obvious and my face looks wrong somehow, like older or harsher or just strange. And even when someone tells me they don’t notice it, I don’t really believe it fully. I start thinking, well, maybe they’re just being kind, or maybe they’re not looking closely, or maybe it only shows in that specific lighting.\n\nSo yes, I guess I can admit there’s a chance I’m making it bigger than it is… but I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it. It’s not like I can just tell myself, “That’s exaggerated,” and then it goes away. It stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hairline and face have changed at all over time, or have they stayed pretty much the same?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s hard to say they’ve changed in a clear way, because the focus is still the same. It keeps coming back to my hairline and my face, especially how my face looks under the restaurant lights. That’s the thing I can’t seem to get away from.\n\nI’ll think, “My hairline looks too noticeable,” or “My face looks old under those lights,” or not even just old—strange, like something is off in a way people can see. And even when coworkers tell me they don’t see it, or they say I look fine, it doesn’t really settle it for me. I might feel relieved for a minute, but then I start questioning it again, like maybe they’re just being polite or maybe they didn’t see me from the right angle.\n\nSo I guess over time it hasn’t gone away or shifted into something else. It’s stayed centered on that same fear: that my hairline and my face make me look old and strange, and I just can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your perception of your hairline and face might be different from how they actually appear to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose there is a possibility. I can say that intellectually, because my coworkers keep telling me they don’t see what I’m talking about. They’ll say my hairline looks normal, or that my face doesn’t look old or strange under the restaurant lights. So I know there’s some chance that what I’m seeing isn’t what other people are seeing.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like just a possibility when I’m in it. It feels obvious to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking that my hairline and the way my face looks under those lights make me look old and strange, even when people around me say they don’t notice anything. I’ll try to take in what they’re saying, but then I start wondering if they’re just being nice, or if they didn’t see me at the right angle, or if maybe the lighting was different that moment.\n\nSo… yes, maybe my perception is different from reality, or different from how others see me. But I don’t trust that enough for it to calm me down. The thought just keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your thoughts about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the main reason I’m here, honestly. I feel like I can’t control it once it starts. I’ll try to tell myself to stop checking or stop thinking about it, but then my mind goes right back to my hairline, or to how my face looks under the restaurant lights.\n\nIt’s like I get stuck on this idea that I look old or strange, and even if my coworkers say, “No, you look fine,” or “I don’t see what you’re talking about,” it doesn’t really shut it off. I might hear them and want to believe them, but then a few minutes later I’m thinking, “Maybe they just didn’t notice,” or “Maybe the lighting wasn’t the same,” or “Maybe they’re trying not to hurt my feelings.”\n\nSo, yes… it feels very hard to control. It’s mostly that it looks too noticeable to me… like the line itself draws attention, especially when I’m under the lights at the restaurant. I’ll catch myself thinking it makes me look older than I should, or like my whole face looks strange because of it.

It’s not just one tiny spot, exactly. It’s more the overall impression—my hairline and my face together, under those lights. I’ll be standing there hosting, and I start imagining that people can see it right away, that something looks off. And then even if a coworker says, “I don’t see what you mean,” I can’t really let it go. I keep thinking maybe they’re just not seeing it the way I am, or they’re trying to be kind.

So the part that bothers me most is how exposed it feels, I guess. Like it makes me look old and strange, and once that thought gets in my head, I can’t stop focusing on it.
