It’s like the hairline makes my whole face look older, harsher, kind of… strange. Like my forehead looks too exposed or uneven, and then under those overhead restaurant lights everything looks worse. My face looks drawn or aged or just not right. I’ll catch myself thinking, “That can’t be how I really look,” and then I’ll look again and it seems even more obvious.\n\nAnd coworkers have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, which almost makes me feel worse in a way, because then I feel like either they’re just being polite or I’m the only one who can see it clearly. But to me it feels very specific. It’s the hairline and the way the lighting hits my face that makes me look old and weird. That’s the part I can’t stop focusing on.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance focused on your hairline and facial features under certain lighting all the time, or does it vary?\n\nPatient: It varies a little, but it’s also kind of always there in the background, if that makes sense. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it — my hairline, and the way my face looks under the restaurant lights. That’s the main thing. I keep feeling like it makes me look old and strange, like something is off about my face.\n\nThe lighting makes it much worse. At the restaurant especially, with those overhead lights, I feel like every flaw is pulled forward, like my hairline is more obvious and my face looks harsher or older. But even when I’m not actually under those lights, I’m still thinking about it. I’ll be anticipating how I’m going to look later, or remembering how I looked the last time, or wondering if other people noticed.\n\nAnd coworkers have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about, but that doesn’t really calm me down. I just think maybe they’re being nice, or maybe they don’t want to say it. So it’s not exactly every second in the same intensity, but it’s there a lot, and the restaurant lighting is one of the things that really sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships or daily activities because of your concerns about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s affected both, honestly. At work it’s become a real problem. I’ve been written up at the restaurant because I’m late or I miss shifts, and I hate even saying that because I’m not someone who wants to be unreliable. But sometimes I get stuck trying to get myself looking acceptable enough to go in, or I start thinking about being under the lights and people seeing me, and I just… I either run late or I can’t make myself go.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve stopped doing things I used to at least try to do. Staff photos are a big one—I avoid them because the idea of everyone seeing a picture of me feels awful. And outside work, I’ve stopped going to gallery events and hikes. It sounds like small stuff maybe, but those were things that connected me to people and to my life, and now I make excuses because I can’t handle being seen that way. Then afterward I feel embarrassed and ashamed, but in the moment avoiding it feels easier than walking in and feeling exposed.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about how you look mostly when you're around other people or can it happen when you're alone too?\n\nPatient: It happens when I’m alone too. It’s definitely worse around other people, or when I know I’m going to be seen under those lights at the restaurant, but it doesn’t just switch off when I’m by myself.\n\nI’ll be alone and still thinking about my hairline, or picturing how my face looks in that lighting, and I get stuck on the idea that it makes me look old and strange. Even if nobody is there looking at me, I’m sort of… looking at myself in my head, if that makes sense. And then when coworkers say they don’t see what I’m talking about, I know I should feel reassured, but I don’t. I keep thinking maybe they just don’t want to say it, or maybe it’s obvious in certain lighting and they’re missing it.\n\nSo yes, people being around makes it more intense, but the worry is there even when I’m alone. It follows me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your appearance might be exaggerated or not entirely based on how you actually look?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows it might be exaggerated, because people at work have told me they don’t see what I’m talking about. My coworkers will say my hairline looks normal, or that my face doesn’t look old or strange under the lights the way I think it does. So I can understand, logically, that maybe I’m not seeing it the way other people are.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel that way to me. It feels very real when I’m looking at myself or even just thinking about being under those restaurant lights. I get stuck on the idea that my hairline is obvious and my face looks wrong somehow, like older or harsher or just strange. And even when someone tells me they don’t notice it, I don’t really believe it fully. I start thinking, well, maybe they’re just being kind, or maybe they’re not looking closely, or maybe it only shows in that specific lighting.\n\nSo yes, I guess I can admit there’s a chance I’m making it bigger than it is… but I’m here because I can’t stop thinking about it. It’s not like I can just tell myself, “That’s exaggerated,” and then it goes away. It stays with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concerns about your hairline and face have changed at all over time, or have they stayed pretty much the same?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s hard to say they’ve changed in a clear way, because the focus is still the same. It keeps coming back to my hairline and my face, especially how my face looks under the restaurant lights. That’s the thing I can’t seem to get away from.\n\nI’ll think, “My hairline looks too noticeable,” or “My face looks old under those lights,” or not even just old—strange, like something is off in a way people can see. And even when coworkers tell me they don’t see it, or they say I look fine, it doesn’t really settle it for me. I might feel relieved for a minute, but then I start questioning it again, like maybe they’re just being polite or maybe they didn’t see me from the right angle.\n\nSo I guess over time it hasn’t gone away or shifted into something else. It’s stayed centered on that same fear: that my hairline and my face make me look old and strange, and I just can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that your perception of your hairline and face might be different from how they actually appear to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I suppose there is a possibility. I can say that intellectually, because my coworkers keep telling me they don’t see what I’m talking about. They’ll say my hairline looks normal, or that my face doesn’t look old or strange under the restaurant lights. So I know there’s some chance that what I’m seeing isn’t what other people are seeing.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like just a possibility when I’m in it. It feels obvious to me. I’m here because I can’t stop thinking that my hairline and the way my face looks under those lights make me look old and strange, even when people around me say they don’t notice anything. I’ll try to take in what they’re saying, but then I start wondering if they’re just being nice, or if they didn’t see me at the right angle, or if maybe the lighting was different that moment.\n\nSo… yes, maybe my perception is different from reality, or different from how others see me. But I don’t trust that enough for it to calm me down. The thought just keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your thoughts about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the main reason I’m here, honestly. I feel like I can’t control it once it starts. I’ll try to tell myself to stop checking or stop thinking about it, but then my mind goes right back to my hairline, or to how my face looks under the restaurant lights.\n\nIt’s like I get stuck on this idea that I look old or strange, and even if my coworkers say, “No, you look fine,” or “I don’t see what you’re talking about,” it doesn’t really shut it off. I might hear them and want to believe them, but then a few minutes later I’m thinking, “Maybe they just didn’t notice,” or “Maybe the lighting wasn’t the same,” or “Maybe they’re trying not to hurt my feelings.”\n\nSo, yes… it feels very hard to control. It’s not just a passing thought. It keeps coming back, and I can’t seem to leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about your hairline that bothers you the most?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that it looks too noticeable to me… like the line itself draws attention, especially when I’m under the lights at the restaurant. I’ll catch myself thinking it makes me look older than I should, or like my whole face looks strange because of it.\n\nIt’s not just one tiny spot, exactly. It’s more the overall impression—my hairline and my face together, under those lights. I’ll be standing there hosting, and I start imagining that people can see it right away, that something looks off. And then even if a coworker says, “I don’t see what you mean,” I can’t really let it go. I keep thinking maybe they’re just not seeing it the way I am, or they’re trying to be kind.\n\nSo the part that bothers me most is how exposed it feels, I guess. Like it makes me look old and strange, and once that thought gets in my head, I can’t stop focusing on it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind about things other than your appearance?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, not in the same way, I don’t think. The thoughts that get stuck are almost always about my appearance—my hairline, my face, how I look under the lights, whether people are noticing.\n\nI do worry about normal things, I guess, like work stuff or whether I said something awkward, but it doesn’t have that same… grip on me. It doesn’t loop the same way for hours or make me keep checking like the appearance thoughts do.\n\nSo if you mean other repeating thoughts that feel as strong or as hard to stop, I don’t think I’ve noticed that. It’s mostly this.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that repeat over and over in your mind about things other than your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think I kind of answered that, but… no, not really, at least not that I can clearly point to. I mean, I have normal worries like anyone does—work, bills, whether I handled something right with a customer—but those don’t take over in the same way.\n\nThe ones that repeat and repeat are about how I look. That’s where my mind gets caught. If it’s something else, I can usually move on or get distracted. But with my appearance, it’s like the thought keeps circling back even when I’m trying to focus on something completely different.\n\nSo, I guess my answer is that I haven’t really noticed that happening with other topics. Not like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your appearance every day?\n\nPatient: Yes. Every day. It’s honestly one of the main reasons I’m here, because it feels like I can’t stop thinking about it.\n\nIt’s mostly my hairline and then how my face looks with it, especially under the lights at the restaurant. I’ll be standing there doing something normal, like greeting people or waiting near the host stand, and then I’ll suddenly get this thought that I look old, or strange, or like something is just visibly wrong with my face. And once I notice it, I don’t really just notice it and move on—it stays there.\n\nEven when people at work tell me they don’t see what I’m talking about, I still keep thinking about it. Part of me knows they might be trying to reassure me, but it doesn’t really settle it in my mind. I keep coming back to, “No, but under these lights it looks different,” or “They’re just not seeing it the way customers do.”\n\nSo yes, it’s daily. It’s not like a once-in-a-while insecurity. It’s there pretty much every day, and it takes up a lot of space in my head."
